On the boundary Carrollian conformal algebra
Authors: Lucas Buzaglo, Xiao He, Tuan Anh Pham, Haijun Tan, Girish S. Vishwa, Kaiming Zhao
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We initiate the mathematical study of the boundary Carrollian conformal algebra (BCCA), an infinite-dimensional Lie algebra recently discovered in the context of Carrollian physics. The BCCA is an intriguing object from both physical and mathematical perspectives, since it is a filtered but not graded Lie algebra. In this paper, we first construct some modules for the BCCA and one of its subalgebras, which we call \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O, by restriction of well-known modules of the BMS3 and Witt algebras, respectively. Along the way, we prove the irreducibility criteria for the so-called induced modules of the BMS3 algebra (which we prefer to call massive modules to avoid ambiguity) and show that this is the same criteria for the irreducibility of the Verma modules of the BMS3 algebra. Interestingly, the modules generated by the action of the BCCA on the generating vector of the massive modules are also irreducible under the same criteria. When these criteria hold, every massive module decomposes into a direct sum of two BCCA-submodules, each of which we conjecture to be indecomposable. Meanwhile, restricting Verma modules to the BCCA and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O leads to free or “almost-free” modules, which are not particularly interesting from a representation-theoretic viewpoint. This motivates the construction of BCCA modules intrinsically. To do this, we go through some structure theory on the BCCA to define a new basis and a decreasing filtration on the algebra, using which we construct Whittaker modules over the BCCA and the subalgebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O and prove criteria for their irreducibility.

Introduction

Over the past two decades, research on infinite-dimensional Lie algebras has enjoyed continuous motivation and inspiration from theoretical physics. Indeed, with the discovery of more examples of infinite-dimensional Lie algebras in physical contexts comes the need to better understand these algebras and their representations. On many occasions, this leads to a productive cycle of progress in both fields, due to the resulting mathematics research informing physics in some manner, which once again motivates another mathematical study. The origin of this paper is no different—we present a first study on another infinite-dimensional Lie algebra that was recently discovered in physics, known as the boundary Carrollian conformal algebra (BCCA), which we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathfrak {b}}$$\end{document}b^. Its centreless version is denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b.

The BCCA was discovered as the symmetry algebra of a Carrollian conformal field theory (CCFT) with boundary in [12], in which it is formulated as a subalgebra of the BMS3 algebra (realised as operators in a quantum field theory) that preserves a choice of boundary on a CCFT on a cylinder. Its original basis consists of the elements \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_n :=L_n - L_{-n}$$\end{document}On:=Ln-L-n (n ≥ 1)n≥1), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_n :=M_n + M_{-n}$$\end{document}Pn:=Mn+M-n (n ≥ 0n≥0), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_M$$\end{document}CM, with its Lie bracket given by1.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{gathered} {[}\mathcal {O}_n,\mathcal {O}_m] = (n - m)\mathcal {O}_{n + m} - (n + m)\mathcal {O}_{n - m}, \\ {[}\mathcal {O}_n, P_m] = (n - m)P_{n + m} + (n + m)P_{n - m} + \frac{1}{6}n(n^2 - 1) \delta _{m,n} C_M, \\ {[}P_n,P_m] = 0, \end{gathered} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[On,Om]=(n-m)On+m-(n+m)On-m,[On,Pm]=(n-m)Pn+m+(n+m)Pn-m+16n(n2-1)δm,nCM,[Pn,Pm]=0,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_n$$\end{document}Ln, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_n$$\end{document}Mn, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_M$$\end{document}CM are elements of the BMS3 algebra defined in Definition 2.3.

In fact, the authors of [12] showed that there exist at least three different boundary-preserving subalgebras of BMS3, but they choose to work with the choice given by (1.1) since it emerges as the algebra of symmetries on the worldsheet of the tensionless open strings, as they demonstrate in their paper. This serves as the primary motivation for our work: understanding the representations of the symmetry algebra of a string theory worldsheet can provide insights into the spectrum of the string theory. Nonetheless, it is interesting to note that one of the other two choices was also discovered in [3] as the worldsheet symmetry algebra of “topological” open strings. This finding presents a further case for the relevance of our work: there may exist connections between the methods developed in this paper and tropical geometry, which in turn could inform string theory.

There are two important features that the BCCA lacks that makes its study both challenging and warranted, these being a grading and a Virasoro subalgebra. Symmetry algebras of string theory worldsheets usually have both, making them amenable to thorough, quantum mechanical treatment using the power tools of vertex operator algebras and semi-infinite cohomology [24, 25, 43, 44, 64]. Today, these toolkits can be coupled with the troves of knowledge on the representation theory of integer-graded infinite-dimensional Lie algebras, to which the past two decades of active research in both physics [1, 13–18, 34, 46, 47, 53] and mathematics [4, 6, 19, 20, 39, 50, 56, 57, 61, 62, 65, 67, 69, 73, 84] have contributed. However, with neither a grading nor a Virasoro subalgebra, the BCCA cannot be studied using these well-established techniques. Hence, understanding the representations of the BCCA presents a novel challenge for both physicists and mathematicians. We therefore develop and employ new approaches that we hope can lay the groundwork for future studies on infinite-dimensional Lie algebras that are not integer-graded while shedding some light on the spectrum of the open tensionless string.

Since the BCCA is defined as a subalgebra of the BMS3 algebra, it is natural to ask whether irreducible modules over BMS3 remain irreducible upon restriction to the BCCA. We therefore start by studying representations of the BCCA and its subalgebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}:={{\,\textrm{span}\,}}\{\mathcal {O}_n \mid n \ge 1\}$$\end{document}O:=span{On∣n≥1} by restricting well-known representations of the Virasoro and BMS3 algebras, namely the tensor density modules, Verma modules, the so-called induced modules introduced in [22] which we prefer to call “massive modules” (see [34]), and the modules \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (\lambda ,a)$$\end{document}Ω(λ,a) introduced in [66]. We work over an arbitrary, characteristic-zero field 𝕜 k to prove the following.

Theorem 1.2

(Propositions 3.13 and 3.15, Corollary 3.16) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M} + \frac{n^2 - 1}{24}c_M \ne 0$$\end{document}M+n2-124cM≠0 for any positive integer n. Then, the massive module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{V}(\textbf{M},\textbf{s},c_L,c_M)$$\end{document}V~(M,s,cL,cM) decomposes into a direct sum of two \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathfrak {b}}$$\end{document}b^-submodules:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \widetilde{V}(\textbf{M},\textbf{s},c_L ,c_M) \cong \widehat{V} \oplus \widehat{W}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}V~(M,s,cL,cM)≅V^⊕W^,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{V}$$\end{document}V^ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{W}$$\end{document}W^ are given by Notation 3.12 and Definition 3.14.

Theorems 1.1 and 1.2 show that many standard examples of BMS3 modules are no longer irreducible upon restriction to the BCCA. In fact, we even see that the tensor density modules and the massive \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms-modules become decomposable when we restrict to the BCCA. On the other hand, Theorem 1.1(4) shows that the massive modules do contain irreducible BCCA-submodules.

We then present the following conjecture as an avenue for further study.

Conjecture 1.3

(Conjecture 3.17) The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathfrak {b}}$$\end{document}b^-modules \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{V}$$\end{document}V^ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{W}$$\end{document}W^ are indecomposable.

On the way to proving Theorem 1.1(4), we also characterise when the massive modules over the BMS3 algebra1 are irreducible, which to our knowledge was not known before.

Theorem 1.4

(Theorem 3.7) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M},\textbf{s},c_L,c_M \in \Bbbk $$\end{document}M,s,cL,cM∈k. Then, the massive module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{V}(\textbf{M},\textbf{s},c_L,c_M)$$\end{document}V~(M,s,cL,cM) over the BMS3 algebra (see Definition 3.6) is irreducible if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M} + \frac{n^2 - 1}{24}c_M \ne 0$$\end{document}M+n2-124cM≠0 for any positive integer n.

This is in line with what is expected based on the gravitational interpretation of the coadjoint orbits of the BMS3 group (see [23] for more details). However, it is surprising that the irreducibility conditions for the massive modules over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms and the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathfrak {b}}$$\end{document}b^-module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(\widehat{\mathfrak {b}})|\textbf{M}, \textbf{s}\rangle $$\end{document}U(b^)|M,s⟩ are the same.

Theorem 1.1 shows that many of the well-known modules over the Virasoro and BMS3 algebras become free, or “almost free” when restricted to the BCCA. From the perspective of representation theory, these are not the most interesting BCCA modules, so we later shift our focus to studying the representation theory of the BCCA intrinsically, by way of Whittaker modules (see Definition 2.9). The main issue is that the presentation of the BCCA given in (1.1) makes it difficult to even define what a Whittaker module over the BCCA should be. For this reason, we spend some time constructing a different basis of the (centreless) BCCA, which better lends itself to the construction of Whittaker modules.

The change of basis is achieved by analysing the subalgebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O, which is a subalgebra of the Witt algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}:={{\,\textrm{Der}\,}}(\Bbbk [t,t^{-1}])$$\end{document}W:=Der(k[t,t-1]), using techniques from [7, 29]. In fact, we work in a more general setting: for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \Bbbk ^*$$\end{document}λ∈k∗ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}n∈Z, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}^{(\lambda )}_n :=L_n - \lambda ^n L_{-n}$$\end{document}On(λ):=Ln-λnL-n, and define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(\lambda ) :={{\,\textrm{span}\,}}\{\mathcal {O}^{(\lambda )}_n \mid n \ge 1\}$$\end{document}O(λ):=span{On(λ)∣n≥1}. It is easy to see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(\lambda )$$\end{document}O(λ) is a Lie algebra with bracket given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\mathcal {O}^{(\lambda )}_n,\mathcal {O}^{(\lambda )}_m] = (n - m)\mathcal {O}^{(\lambda )}_{n + m} - \lambda ^m (n + m) \mathcal {O}^{(\lambda )}_{n - m} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[On(λ),Om(λ)]=(n-m)On+m(λ)-λm(n+m)On-m(λ)for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n,m \in \mathbb {Z}_+$$\end{document}n,m∈Z+ (see Lemma 4.14).

Theorem 1.5

(Theorem 4.16, Proposition 4.22, and Corollary 4.23) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda , \mu \in \Bbbk ^*$$\end{document}λ,μ∈k∗. Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(\lambda )$$\end{document}O(λ) has another basis \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u^{(\lambda )}_n \mid n \ge 1\}$$\end{document}{un(λ)∣n≥1} with Lie bracket\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}u^{(\lambda )}_n, u^{(\lambda )}_m] = (n - m)(u^{(\lambda )}_{n + m} - 4\lambda u^{(\lambda )}_{n + m - 2}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[un(λ),um(λ)]=(n-m)(un+m(λ)-4λun+m-2(λ)).Consequently, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(\lambda ) \cong \mathcal {O}(\mu )$$\end{document}O(λ)≅O(μ) if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{\frac{\lambda }{\mu }} \in \Bbbk $$\end{document}λμ∈k. In particular, if 𝕜 k is algebraically closed (for example, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk = \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}k=C), then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(\lambda ) \cong \mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O(λ)≅O for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \Bbbk $$\end{document}λ∈k. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk = \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}k=R, then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(\lambda ) \cong {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \mathcal {O}, & {\text {if }} \lambda > 0, \\ \mathcal {O}(-1), & {\text {if }} \lambda < 0, \end{array}\right. }$$\end{document}O(λ)≅O,ifλ>0,O(-1),ifλ<0,but \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(-1) \not \cong \mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O(-1)≇O.

We further present some consequences of Theorem 1.5 in Sect. 4. For example, it becomes easy to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O is not a simple Lie algebra (in fact, it is not even perfect)—see Corollary 4.19. Certainly, the same is true for both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathfrak {b}}$$\end{document}b^.

Theorem 1.5 tells us that the Lie subalgebras \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(-1)$$\end{document}O(-1) of the Witt algebra are isomorphic as complex Lie algebras but not as real ones. This leads to an interesting physical implication. In [12], the authors start with the BMS3 algebra realised as vector fields which generate the infinitesimal transformations that leave the Carrollian structure on a cylinder (given by a symmetric (0, 2)-tensor field h that is corank-1 everywhere on the cylinder and a nowhere-vanishing vector field ξ ξ such that \ker hkerh is generated by ξ ξ) invariant up to an overall conformal factor. Using this, the Lie algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O was constructed as the subalgebra of the Witt algebra consisting of the vector fields that preserve a choice of boundaries on the cylinder at 0 and π π. Likewise, the Lie algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(-1)$$\end{document}O(-1) can be viewed as the analogous Lie algebra when the boundaries are instead placed at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tfrac{\pi }{2}$$\end{document}π2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tfrac{3\pi }{2}$$\end{document}3π2. But one can always recover the original boundary conditions by translating the periodic coordinate by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tfrac{\pi }{2}$$\end{document}π2, which puts back in the setting where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O is the distinguished boundary-preserving subalgebra. This equivalence must be reflected algebraically too, which indicates that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(-1)$$\end{document}O(-1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O must be isomorphic Lie algebras. Thus, by Theorem 1.5, we confirm that the authors [12] must be working over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}$$\end{document}C and not \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R. We provide details of this observation in Remark 4.24.

Having constructed the alternate basis of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O in Theorem 1.5, we then extend it to a new basis of the full centreless BCCA.

Theorem 1.6

(Corollary 4.34) There is another basis of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u_n, v_m \mid n \ge 1, m \ge 0\}$$\end{document}{un,vm∣n≥1,m≥0}. The bracket of the centreless BCCA with this basis is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}u_n,u_m]&= (n - m)(u_{n + m} - 4u_{n + m - 2}) & (n,m \ge 1), \\ {[}u_n,v_m]&= (n - m)v_{n + m} - 4(n - m - 1)v_{n + m - 2} & (n \ge 1, m \ge 0), \\ {[}v_n,v_m]&= 0 & (n,m \ge 0). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[un,um]=(n-m)(un+m-4un+m-2)(n,m≥1),[un,vm]=(n-m)vn+m-4(n-m-1)vn+m-2(n≥1,m≥0),[vn,vm]=0(n,m≥0).

Using the new basis for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b from Theorem 1.6, we can easily construct a filtration for the centreless BCCA, allowing us to define and study Whittaker modules for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b, since filtered Lie algebras always have Whittaker modules (see Lemma 2.11). For convenience, we shift the basis as follows: let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_n :=u_{n + 2}$$\end{document}Un:=un+2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_n :=v_{n + 1}$$\end{document}Vn:=vn+1 for n ≥ -1n≥-1.

Proposition 1.7

(Proposition 5.2) For n ≥ -1n≥-1, define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}:={{\,\textrm{span}\,}}\{\mathcal {U}_k \mid k \ge n\}, \quad \mathcal {F}_n\mathfrak {b}:={{\,\textrm{span}\,}}\{\mathcal {U}_k, \mathcal {V}_k \mid k \ge n\}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}FnO:=span{Uk∣k≥n},Fnb:=span{Uk,Vk∣k≥n}.Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}$$\end{document}F is a filtration of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b.

Consequently, we define Whittaker functions on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b to be Lie algebra homomorphisms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _n :\mathcal {F}_n \mathfrak {b}\rightarrow \Bbbk $$\end{document}ψn:Fnb→k for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N. The universal Whittaker module of type \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _n$$\end{document}ψn is defined to be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\psi _n} :={{\,\textrm{Ind}\,}}_{\mathcal {F}_n \mathfrak {b}}^\mathfrak {b}\Bbbk _{\psi _n}$$\end{document}Mψn:=IndFnbbkψn. Similar definitions can be made for the subalgebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O.

If n = 0n=0, then the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _0}$$\end{document}Mφ0 is irreducible if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _0(\mathcal {U}_{2}) \ne 4 \varphi _0(\mathcal {U}_{0})$$\end{document}φ0(U2)≠4φ0(U0).

If n ≥ 1n≥1, then the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _n}$$\end{document}Mφn is irreducible if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n + 1}) \ne 4 \varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n - 1})$$\end{document}φn(U2n+1)≠4φn(U2n-1) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n + 2}) \ne 4\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n})$$\end{document}φn(U2n+2)≠4φn(U2n).

If n ≥ 1n≥1, then the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _n}$$\end{document}Mφn is irreducible if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n + 1}) \ne 4 \varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n - 1})$$\end{document}φn(U2n+1)≠4φn(U2n-1) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n + 2}) \ne 4\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n})$$\end{document}φn(U2n+2)≠4φn(U2n).

If n = 0n=0, then the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b-module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\psi _0}$$\end{document}Mψ0 is irreducible if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _0(\mathcal {V}_0) \ne 0$$\end{document}ψ0(V0)≠0.

If n = 0n=0, then the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b-module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\psi _0}$$\end{document}Mψ0 is irreducible if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _0(\mathcal {V}_0) \ne 0$$\end{document}ψ0(V0)≠0.

If n ≥ 1n≥1, then the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b-module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}Mψn is irreducible if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _n(\mathcal {V}_{2n}) \ne 0$$\end{document}ψn(V2n)≠0 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _n(\mathcal {V}_{2n - 1}) \ne 0$$\end{document}ψn(V2n-1)≠0.

If n ≥ 1n≥1, then the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b-module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}Mψn is irreducible if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _n(\mathcal {V}_{2n}) \ne 0$$\end{document}ψn(V2n)≠0 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _n(\mathcal {V}_{2n - 1}) \ne 0$$\end{document}ψn(V2n-1)≠0.

The proof of Theorem 1.8(1) and (2) uses a version of the classical Kirillov orbit method [58] for the Virasoro algebra developed in [71, 81]. For parts (3) and (4), our proof is more direct.

Unfortunately, there is no way to extend the filtration to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathfrak {b}}$$\end{document}b^, at least not while keeping the nice properties of the filtration (particularly weak convergence—see Definition 2.10). We elaborate on this in Remark 4.37. This means that we are currently unable to define Whittaker modules for the BCCA on which the centre acts nontrivially. It would be interesting to further explore representations of the BCCA on which the centre acts nontrivially.

Certainly, there exists strong physical and mathematical motivation for this paper. The former arises from the fact that an infinite-dimensional Lie algebra which is not integer-graded has made an appearance in a modern theoretical physics setting, meaning that we need to develop the tools needed to study such objects thoroughly, starting with the BCCA as the inaugural case study. The latter comes from the desire to understand infinite-dimensional subalgebras of infinite-dimensional Lie algebras more thoroughly.

This paper is organised as follows: In Sect. 2, we set up notation, present key definitions along with minor or known results, and introduce the main objects of study of this paper. Section 3 constructs modules of the BCCA and one of its subalgebras by restricting well-known modules of the BMS3 and Virasoro algebras. This would be the naive approach to the studying the representations of the BCCA based on its physical origins in [12]. The emergence of free and “almost-free” modules motivates the need to study modules of the BCCA intrinsically. This requires the change of basis presented in Sect. 4, from which we can construct a decreasing filtration of the BCCA and compute derived subalgebras, as done in Sect. 5. All this effort pays off in Sect. 6, in which we construct the universal Whittaker modules over the BCCA and its subalgebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O and determine criteria for their irreducibility. Finally, in Sect. 7, we present a myriad of future directions of work that can be undertaken on the representation theory of the BCCA, from both physical and mathematical perspectives.

The boundary Carrollian conformal algebra

We define the fundamental objects of interest in this paper and present the construction of the boundary Carrollian conformal algebra (BCCA).

Definition 2.1

The Witt algebra is defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}:={{\,\textrm{Der}\,}}(\Bbbk [t,t^{-1}]) = \Bbbk [t,t^{-1}]\partial $$\end{document}W:=Der(k[t,t-1])=k[t,t-1]∂, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial :=\frac{d}{dt}$$\end{document}∂:=ddt. The standard basis of the Witt algebra is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_n :=-t^{n + 1}\partial $$\end{document}Ln:=-tn+1∂ (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \in \mathbb {Z})$$\end{document}n∈Z), and its Lie bracket is given by:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}f\partial , g\partial ] = (fg' - f'g)\partial , \qquad [L_n,L_m] = (n - m)L_{n + m}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[f∂,g∂]=(fg′-f′g)∂,[Ln,Lm]=(n-m)Ln+m,for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f,g \in \Bbbk [t,t^{-1}]$$\end{document}f,g∈k[t,t-1] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n,m \in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}n,m∈Z. The Witt algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W can be seen as the Lie algebra of algebraic vector fields on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk ^*$$\end{document}k∗.

The Virasoro algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}$$\end{document}Vir is the universal central extension of the Witt algebra. As a vector space, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}= \mathcal {W}\oplus \Bbbk C$$\end{document}Vir=W⊕kC with bracket given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & {[}f\partial ,g\partial ] = (fg' - f'g)\partial + \frac{1}{12} \operatorname {Res}_0(f'g'')C, \\ & {[}L_n,L_m] = (n - m)L_{n + m} + \frac{1}{12} n(n^2 - 1) \delta _{m,-n} C, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[f∂,g∂]=(fg′-f′g)∂+112Res0(f′g′′)C,[Ln,Lm]=(n-m)Ln+m+112n(n2-1)δm,-nC,for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f,g \in \Bbbk [t,t^{-1}]$$\end{document}f,g∈k[t,t-1], where C is central, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {Res}_0(h)$$\end{document}Res0(h) is the residue of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h \in \Bbbk [t,t^{-1}]$$\end{document}h∈k[t,t-1] at zero, or in other words, the coefficient of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t^{-1}$$\end{document}t-1 in h.

The one-sided Witt algebra is defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_1 :={{\,\textrm{Der}\,}}(\Bbbk [t]) = \Bbbk [t]\partial $$\end{document}W1:=Der(k[t])=k[t]∂. Equivalently, it can be viewed as the subalgebra of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W spanned by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{L_n \mid n \ge -1\}$$\end{document}{Ln∣n≥-1} and it is the Lie algebra of algebraic vector fields on 𝕜 k.

We often switch between the basis-free notation f∂ f∂ for elements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W and the basis notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_n$$\end{document}Ln.

Definition 2.2

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a,b \in \Bbbk $$\end{document}a,b∈k, define a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W-module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I(a,b) = {{\,\textrm{span}\,}}\{I_n \mid n \in \mathbb {Z}\}$$\end{document}I(a,b)=span{In∣n∈Z} as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L_n \cdot I_m = - (a + bn + m)I_{n + m}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ln·Im=-(a+bn+m)In+m.The modules I(a, b) are known as tensor density modules. In basis-free notation, we define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I(a,b) :=t^{a - b}\Bbbk [t,t^{-1}] \ dt^b$$\end{document}I(a,b):=ta-bk[t,t-1]dtb with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W-action given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f\partial \cdot (g \ dt^b) = (fg' + bf'g)dt^b. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f∂·(gdtb)=(fg′+bf′g)dtb.With this perspective, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_n :=- t^{n + a - b} \ dt^b$$\end{document}In:=-tn+a-bdtb. Note that I(0,-1)I(0,-1) is the adjoint module of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W.

We may then construct the two-parameter family of Lie algebras \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}(a,b) :=\mathcal {W}\ltimes I(a,b)$$\end{document}W(a,b):=W⋉I(a,b). The special case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}(0,-1)$$\end{document}W(0,-1) is the centreless version of the BMS3 algebra, presented in the next definition. The BMS3 algebra first appeared in physics in [2, 10], and in mathematics, it was introduced as the W(2, 2) algebra2 by Zhang and Dong but with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_L = C_M$$\end{document}CL=CM [84].

Definition 2.3

The BMS3 algebra, denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms, is the universal central extension of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}(0,-1)$$\end{document}W(0,-1). It is the infinite-dimensional Lie algebra spanned by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{L_n,M_n \mid n \in \mathbb {Z}\}$$\end{document}{Ln,Mn∣n∈Z} and two central elements \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_L, C_M$$\end{document}CL,CM, with Lie bracket2.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{gathered} {[}L_n,L_m] = (n - m) L_{m + n} + \frac{1}{12} n(n^2 - 1) \delta _{m,-n} C_L, \\ {[}L_n,M_m] = (n - m) M_{m + n} + \frac{1}{12} n(n^2 - 1)\delta _{m,-n} C_M, \\ {[}M_n,M_m] = 0. \end{gathered} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[Ln,Lm]=(n-m)Lm+n+112n(n2-1)δm,-nCL,[Ln,Mm]=(n-m)Mm+n+112n(n2-1)δm,-nCM,[Mn,Mm]=0.Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{L_n \mid n\in \mathbb {Z}\}$$\end{document}{Ln∣n∈Z} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_L$$\end{document}CL form a basis of a Virasoro subalgebra and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{M_n \mid n \in \mathbb {Z}\}$$\end{document}{Mn∣n∈Z} is the basis of I(0,-1)I(0,-1) presented in Definition 2.2.

We now define the BCCA in its original form given by [12, Equation (7)]. This will be the main object of study of our paper.

Definition 2.4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_n:=L_n - L_{-n}$$\end{document}On:=Ln-L-n and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_n :=M_n + M_{-n}$$\end{document}Pn:=Mn+M-n for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}n∈Z, which means that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_n = -\mathcal {O}_{-n}$$\end{document}On=-O-n and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_n = \mathcal {P}_{-n}$$\end{document}Pn=P-n for all n≥ 1n≥1. The boundary Carrollian conformal algebra (BCCA), denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathfrak {b}}$$\end{document}b^, is the Lie subalgebra of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms with basis \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {O}_n, P_m, C_M \mid n \ge 1,\ m \ge 0\}$$\end{document}{On,Pm,CM∣n≥1,m≥0} and Lie bracket2.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{gathered} {[}\mathcal {O}_n,\mathcal {O}_m] = (n - m)\mathcal {O}_{n + m} - (n + m)\mathcal {O}_{n - m}, \\ {[}\mathcal {O}_n, P_m] = (n - m)P_{n + m} + (n + m)P_{n - m} + \frac{1}{6}n(n^2 - 1) \delta _{m,n} C_M, \\ {[}P_n,P_m] = 0. \end{gathered} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[On,Om]=(n-m)On+m-(n+m)On-m,[On,Pm]=(n-m)Pn+m+(n+m)Pn-m+16n(n2-1)δm,nCM,[Pn,Pm]=0.The centreless BCCA (in other words, setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_M = 0$$\end{document}CM=0) is denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b.

The latter half of this paper mostly focuses on the centreless BCCA. This is because it is not clear how one could define Whittaker modules over the BCCA with centre. We elaborate on this in Remark 4.37.

Remark 2.5

The Lie algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b is the fixed subalgebra of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}(0,-1)$$\end{document}W(0,-1) under the automorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_n \mapsto -L_{-n},\, M_n \mapsto M_{-n}$$\end{document}Ln↦-L-n,Mn↦M-n.

First, we seek to write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b as a semi-direct sum in a similar manner to how the centreless BMS3 algebra can be written as the semi-direct sum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}(0,-1) :\!\!-\mathcal {W}\ltimes I(0,-1)$$\end{document}W(0,-1):-W⋉I(0,-1). To do this, we define the following subspaces of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W and I(a, b).

Definition 2.6

The subalgebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}\subseteq \mathcal {W}$$\end{document}O⊆W is spanned by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {O}_n \mid n \ge 1\}$$\end{document}{On∣n≥1}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_n :=L_n - L_{-n}$$\end{document}On:=Ln-L-n. Its Lie bracket is given by2.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\mathcal {O}_n, \mathcal {O}_m] = (n - m)\mathcal {O}_{n + m} - (n + m)\mathcal {O}_{n - m}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[On,Om]=(n-m)On+m-(n+m)On-m.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I(0,b) = {{\,\textrm{span}\,}}\{I_n \mid n \in \mathbb {Z}\}$$\end{document}I(0,b)=span{In∣n∈Z} be the tensor density modules from Definition 2.2. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P^{(b)}_n :=I_n + I_{-n}$$\end{document}Pn(b):=In+I-n and construct \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_b :={{\,\textrm{span}\,}}\{P^{(b)}_n \mid n \in \mathbb {N}\}$$\end{document}Pb:=span{Pn(b)∣n∈N}. Likewise, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{P}^{(b)}_n :=I_n - I_{-n}$$\end{document}P~n(b):=In-I-n and construct \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\mathcal {P}}_b :={{\,\textrm{span}\,}}\{\widetilde{P}^{(b)}_n \mid n \in \mathbb {N}^+\}$$\end{document}P~b:=span{P~n(b)∣n∈N+}. The vector spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_b$$\end{document}Pb and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\mathcal {P}}_b$$\end{document}P~b are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-modules under the following actions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O inherited from the action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W on I(0, b):2.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\mathcal {O}_n \cdot P_m^{(b)} = -(bn + m) P_{n + m}^{(b)} - (bn - m) P_{n-m}^{(b)} \quad (n \ge 1, m \ge 0) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}On·Pm(b)=-(bn+m)Pn+m(b)-(bn-m)Pn-m(b)(n≥1,m≥0)2.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\mathcal {O}_n \cdot \widetilde{P}_m^{(b)} = -(bn + m) \widetilde{P}_{n + m}^{(b)} - (bn - m) \widetilde{P}_{n-m}^{(b)} \quad (n,m \ge 1). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}On·P~m(b)=-(bn+m)P~n+m(b)-(bn-m)P~n-m(b)(n,m≥1).For b = -1b=-1, we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}:=\mathcal {P}_{-1}$$\end{document}P:=P-1. Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\mathcal {P}}_{-1}$$\end{document}P~-1 is the adjoint module of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O.

It is easy to see that I(0, b) decomposes as a direct sum of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_b$$\end{document}Pb and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\mathcal {P}}_b$$\end{document}P~b as an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-module.

Lemma 2.7

As \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-modules, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I(0,b)\cong \mathcal {P}_b \oplus \widetilde{\mathcal {P}}_b$$\end{document}I(0,b)≅Pb⊕P~b.

Proof

There exists an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-module homomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho :I(0,b)\rightarrow \mathcal {P}_b$$\end{document}ρ:I(0,b)→Pb given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho (I_n) = P_n$$\end{document}ρ(In)=Pn for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}n∈Z. We then have a short exact sequence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-modules2.6The inclusion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_b \hookrightarrow I(0,b)$$\end{document}Pb↪I(0,b) is a section of the short exact sequence, and thus, the sequence is split. It follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I(0,b)\cong \mathcal {P}_b \oplus \widetilde{\mathcal {P}}_b$$\end{document}I(0,b)≅Pb⊕P~b for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b\in \Bbbk $$\end{document}b∈k. □ □

We therefore see that Definition 2.4 can be succinctly summarised as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}:=\mathcal {O}\ltimes \mathcal {P}_{-1} = \mathcal {O}\ltimes \mathcal {P}$$\end{document}b:=O⋉P-1=O⋉P, analogous to how the centreless BMS3 algebra is defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}(0,-1) :=\mathcal {W}\ltimes I(0,-1)$$\end{document}W(0,-1):=W⋉I(0,-1). The higher spin (centreless) BCCAs can be similarly defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}\ltimes \mathcal {P}_b$$\end{document}O⋉Pb for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b \in \Bbbk $$\end{document}b∈k. See the commentary around [12, Equation (26)] for more details. We only focus on the case b = -1b=-1 in this paper, but our techniques could also be applied to these higher spin algebras.

Whittaker modules

Whittaker modules were first discovered for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {sl}_2$$\end{document}sl2 by Arnal and Pinczon in [5], following which Kostant defined them for finite-dimensional complex semisimple Lie algebras in [59]. This was then generalised to arbitrary Lie algebras with triangular decomposition. Today, Whittaker modules are defined more generally via the notion of “Whittaker pairs” [21, 69] and play an important role in the representation theory of various infinite-dimensional Lie algebras [4, 33, 35–37, 39, 51, 60, 63, 70, 82, 85].

In this paper, we construct Whittaker modules of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b based on the definition given in [21, Section 3.2] and [69, Section 5.1], thereby presenting explicit examples of their constructions for infinite-dimensional Lie algebras that are not integer-graded.

Definition 2.8

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {g}$$\end{document}g be a Lie algebra. Define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {g}^0 :\!\!-\mathfrak {g}$$\end{document}g0:-g and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {g}^{k+1}:\!\!-[\mathfrak {g}^k, \mathfrak {g}]$$\end{document}gk+1:-[gk,g] for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N.
