\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{gr}\,}}_\mathcal {F}\mathfrak {b}\cong \mathbb {W}_1 \ltimes {{\,\textrm{ad}\,}}(\mathbb {W}_1)$$\end{document}grFb≅W1⋉ad(W1), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{ad}\,}}(\mathbb {W}_1)$$\end{document}ad(W1) is another copy of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}W1, viewed as the adjoint module of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}W1 with abelian Lie bracket.

Proof

We have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\mathcal {U}_n + \mathcal {F}_{n+1}\mathcal {O}, \mathcal {U}_m + \mathcal {F}_{m + 1}\mathcal {O}]&= [\mathcal {U}_n,\mathcal {U}_m]+\mathcal {F}_{m+n+1}\mathcal {O}\\&= -4(n - m)\mathcal {U}_{n + m} + \mathcal {F}_{n + m + 1}\mathcal {O}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[Un+Fn+1O,Um+Fm+1O]=[Un,Um]+Fm+n+1O=-4(n-m)Un+m+Fn+m+1O.Thus, the Lie algebra homomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_n + \mathcal {F}_{n + 1}\mathcal {O}\mapsto -4L_n$$\end{document}Un+Fn+1O↦-4Ln establishes the isomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{gr}\,}}_\mathcal {F}\mathcal {O}\cong \mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}grFO≅W1. Similarly, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\mathcal {U}_n + \mathcal {F}_{n + 1}\mathfrak {b}, \mathcal {V}_m + \mathcal {F}_{m + 1}\mathfrak {b}]&= [\mathcal {U}_n,\mathcal {V}_m]+\mathcal {F}_{n+m+1}\mathfrak {b}\\&= -4(n - m)\mathcal {V}_{n + m} + \mathcal {F}_{n + m + 1}\mathfrak {b}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[Un+Fn+1b,Vm+Fm+1b]=[Un,Vm]+Fn+m+1b=-4(n-m)Vn+m+Fn+m+1b.It is now clear that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{gr}\,}}_\mathcal {F}\mathfrak {b}\cong \mathbb {W}_1 \ltimes {{\,\textrm{ad}\,}}(\mathbb {W}_1)$$\end{document}grFb≅W1⋉ad(W1) using a similar isomorphism to the above, as required. □ □

The derived subalgebras of the filtered subspaces

To study Whittaker functions on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b, we must study their Whittaker functions. By definition, these are Lie algebra homomorphisms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n \mathcal {O}\rightarrow \Bbbk $$\end{document}FnO→k or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n \mathfrak {b}\rightarrow \Bbbk $$\end{document}Fnb→k, for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N. Since 𝕜 k is an abelian Lie algebra, these homomorphisms must necessarily vanish on the derived subalgebras \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathcal {F}_n \mathcal {O}, \mathcal {F}_n \mathcal {O}]$$\end{document}[FnO,FnO] or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathcal {F}_n \mathfrak {b}, \mathcal {F}_n \mathfrak {b}]$$\end{document}[Fnb,Fnb]. We compute these in this section.

First, note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O},\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}] \subseteq \mathcal {F}_{2n + 1}\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}[FnO,FnO]⊆F2n+1O, by (5.1). We now prove that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O},\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}]$$\end{document}[FnO,FnO] is spanned by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_{2n + 2 + k} - 4 \, \mathcal {U}_{2n + k}$$\end{document}U2n+2+k-4U2n+k for k ≥ 1k≥1.

Lemma 5.4

For all n ≥ -1n≥-1, the derived subalgebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O},\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}]$$\end{document}[FnO,FnO] has the following form:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O},\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}] = (s^{2n + 4} - 4\,s^{2n + 2})\mathcal {O}= (s^{2n + 4} - 4\,s^{2n + 2})(s^2 - 4)\Bbbk [s]\partial _s. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[FnO,FnO]=(s2n+4-4s2n+2)O=(s2n+4-4s2n+2)(s2-4)k[s]∂s.Consequently, the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {U}_{2n + 3 + k} - 4 \, \mathcal {U}_{2n + 1 + k} \mid k \in \mathbb {N}\}$$\end{document}{U2n+3+k-4U2n+1+k∣k∈N} is a basis for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O},\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}]$$\end{document}[FnO,FnO].

Proof

Recall from Theorem 4.16 that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}= (s^2 - 4)\Bbbk [s]\partial _s$$\end{document}O=(s2-4)k[s]∂s, and that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}= s^{n + 1}\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}FnO=sn+1O by definition. Therefore,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O},\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}] = [s^{n + 1}(s^2 - 4)\Bbbk [s]\partial _s, s^{n + 1}(s^2 - 4)\Bbbk [s]\partial _s] = \Big (s^{n + 1}(s^2 - 4)\Big )^2\Bbbk [s]\partial _s, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[FnO,FnO]=[sn+1(s2-4)k[s]∂s,sn+1(s2-4)k[s]∂s]=(sn+1(s2-4))2k[s]∂s,by (4.1). Simplifying, we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O},\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}]= & \Big (s^{n + 1}(s^2 - 4)\Big )^2\Bbbk [s]\partial _s = s^{2n + 2}(s^2 - 4)^2\Bbbk [s]\partial _s \\= & (s^{2n + 4} - 4s^{2n + 2})(s^2 - 4)\Bbbk [s]\partial _s. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[FnO,FnO]=(sn+1(s2-4))2k[s]∂s=s2n+2(s2-4)2k[s]∂s=(s2n+4-4s2n+2)(s2-4)k[s]∂s.Noting that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {U}_{2n + 3 + k} - 4 \, \mathcal {U}_{2n + 1 + k} = (s^{2n + 4 + k} - 4\,s^{2n + 2 + k})(s^2 - 4)\partial _s, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}U2n+3+k-4U2n+1+k=(s2n+4+k-4s2n+2+k)(s2-4)∂s,it is now clear that the result follows. □ □

We now proceed as in Lemma 5.4 to compute the derived subalgebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathcal {F}_n\mathfrak {b},\mathcal {F}_n\mathfrak {b}]$$\end{document}[Fnb,Fnb] for the filtration of the full BCCA. Similarly to the above, (5.2) implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}\cdot \mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {P}\subseteq \mathcal {F}_{2n + 1}\mathcal {P}$$\end{document}FnO·FnP⊆F2n+1P. In fact, this is an equality: we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}\cdot \mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {P}= \mathcal {F}_{2n + 1} \mathcal {P}$$\end{document}FnO·FnP=F2n+1P, as we prove next.

Lemma 5.5

For all n ≥ -1n≥-1, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O},\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {P}] = \mathcal {F}_{2n + 1}\mathcal {P}$$\end{document}[FnO,FnP]=F2n+1P. Consequently,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\mathcal {F}_n\mathfrak {b},\mathcal {F}_n\mathfrak {b}] = [\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O},\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}] \ltimes \mathcal {F}_{2n + 1}\mathcal {P}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[Fnb,Fnb]=[FnO,FnO]⋉F2n+1P,and therefore the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {U}_{2n + 3 + k} - 4 \, \mathcal {U}_{2n + 1 + k}, \mathcal {V}_{2n + 1 + k} \mid k \in \mathbb {N}\}$$\end{document}{U2n+3+k-4U2n+1+k,V2n+1+k∣k∈N} is a basis for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathcal {F}_n\mathfrak {b},\mathcal {F}_n\mathfrak {b}]$$\end{document}[Fnb,Fnb].

Proof

For all m ≥ nm≥n, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {V}_{2m + 1}&= \frac{1}{4}[\mathcal {U}_m,\mathcal {V}_{m + 1}] \in [\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O},\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {P}], \\ \mathcal {V}_{2m + 2}&= [\mathcal {U}_m,\mathcal {V}_m] \in [\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O},\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {P}], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}V2m+1=14[Um,Vm+1]∈[FnO,FnP],V2m+2=[Um,Vm]∈[FnO,FnP],by (5.2). The result follows. □ □

The abelianisations of the filtered subspaces

We finish this section by computing the dimensions of the abelianisations of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n \mathcal {O}$$\end{document}FnO and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n \mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}Fnb. This computation will tell us exactly how many degrees of freedom one has when defining a Whittaker function on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O or on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b. The following easy observation will be useful for this purpose.

Lemma 5.6

For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g \in \Bbbk [t]$$\end{document}g∈k[t], the codimension of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}gW1 in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}W1 is \deg (g)deg(g). □ □

Of course, Lemma 5.6 also implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(s)\Bbbk [s]\partial _s$$\end{document}g(s)k[s]∂s has codimension \deg (g)deg(g) in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk [s]\partial _s$$\end{document}k[s]∂s, via the change of variables s ↦ ts↦t.

Proposition 5.7

We have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dim \left( \frac{\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}}{[\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O},\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}]}\right) = n + 3, \quad \dim \left( \frac{\mathcal {F}_n\mathfrak {b}}{[\mathcal {F}_n\mathfrak {b},\mathcal {F}_n\mathfrak {b}]}\right) = 2n + 4, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dimFnO[FnO,FnO]=n+3,dimFnb[Fnb,Fnb]=2n+4,for all n ≥ -1n≥-1.

Proof

By Theorem 4.16, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}= s^{n + 1}(s^2 - 4)\Bbbk [s]\partial _s. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}FnO=sn+1(s2-4)k[s]∂s.This is a subalgebra of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk [s]\partial _s$$\end{document}k[s]∂s of codimension n + 3n+3, by Lemma 5.6. On the other hand, Lemma 5.6 also implies that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O},\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}] = \Big (s^{n + 1}(s^2 - 4)\Big )^2\Bbbk [s]\partial _s \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[FnO,FnO]=(sn+1(s2-4))2k[s]∂sis a subalgebra of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk [s]\partial _s$$\end{document}k[s]∂s of codimension 2n + 62n+6. Therefore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O},\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}]$$\end{document}[FnO,FnO] has codimension n + 3n+3 in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}FnO, as required.

For the second part of the result, we observe that the codimension of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_m\mathcal {P}$$\end{document}FmP in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {P}$$\end{document}FnP is m - nm-n, for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m \ge n \ge -1$$\end{document}m≥n≥-1. Now, Lemmas 5.5 and 5.6 imply that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dim \left( \frac{\mathcal {F}_n\mathfrak {b}}{[\mathcal {F}_n\mathfrak {b},\mathcal {F}_n\mathfrak {b}]}\right) = \dim \left( \frac{\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}}{[\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O},\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}]}\right) + \dim (\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {P}/\mathcal {F}_{2n + 1}\mathcal {P}) = n + 3 + n + 1 = 2n + 4, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dimFnb[Fnb,Fnb]=dimFnO[FnO,FnO]+dim(FnP/F2n+1P)=n+3+n+1=2n+4,which concludes the proof. □ □

More precisely, write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{x}$$\end{document}x¯ for the image of x in the abelianisation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n \mathfrak {b}/[\mathcal {F}_n \mathfrak {b}, \mathcal {F}_n \mathfrak {b}]$$\end{document}Fnb/[Fnb,Fnb] or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n \mathcal {O}/[\mathcal {F}_n \mathcal {O}, \mathcal {F}_n \mathcal {O}]$$\end{document}FnO/[FnO,FnO], where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in \mathcal {F}_n\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}x∈Fnb or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in \mathcal {F}_n \mathcal {O}$$\end{document}x∈FnO, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}n∈Z. Then Proposition 5.7 implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\overline{\mathcal {U}}_n, \dots , \overline{\mathcal {U}}_{2n+2}\}$$\end{document}{U¯n,⋯,U¯2n+2} forms a basis of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n \mathcal {O}/[\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O},\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}]$$\end{document}FnO/[FnO,FnO], and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\overline{\mathcal {U}}_n, \dots , \overline{\mathcal {U}}_{2n+2}\} \cup \{\overline{\mathcal {V}}_n, \dots , \overline{\mathcal {V}}_{2n}\}$$\end{document}{U¯n,⋯,U¯2n+2}∪{V¯n,⋯,V¯2n} forms a basis of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n \mathfrak {b}/[\mathcal {F}_n\mathfrak {b},\mathcal {F}_n\mathfrak {b}]$$\end{document}Fnb/[Fnb,Fnb].

The simplest application of Proposition 5.7 is that we can completely classify all one-dimensional representations of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b.

Definition 5.8

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda , \mu \in \Bbbk $$\end{document}λ,μ∈k, and define the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk _{\lambda ,\mu }$$\end{document}kλ,μ as follows: as a vector space, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk _{\lambda ,\mu } = \Bbbk 1_{\lambda ,\mu }$$\end{document}kλ,μ=k1λ,μ, and the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-action is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {U}_{-1} \cdot 1_{\lambda ,\mu } = \lambda 1_{\lambda ,\mu }, \quad \mathcal {U}_0 \cdot 1_{\lambda ,\mu } = \mu 1_{\lambda ,\mu }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}U-1·1λ,μ=λ1λ,μ,U0·1λ,μ=μ1λ,μ.The action of the other basis elements is determined by these and the formula\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {U}_n \cdot 1_{\lambda ,\mu } = 4 \, \mathcal {U}_{n - 2} \cdot 1_{\lambda ,\mu } \quad (n \ge 1). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Un·1λ,μ=4Un-2·1λ,μ(n≥1).

For example, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_1 \cdot 1_{\lambda ,\mu } = 4\lambda 1_{\lambda ,\mu }$$\end{document}U1·1λ,μ=4λ1λ,μ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_2 \cdot 1_{\lambda ,\mu } = 4\mu 1_{\lambda ,\mu }$$\end{document}U2·1λ,μ=4μ1λ,μ. In fact, all one-dimensional representations of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b are of the above form, which is an immediate consequence of Lemmas 5.4 and 5.5, and Proposition 5.7.

Corollary 5.9

If M is a one-dimensional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-representation, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M \cong \Bbbk _{\lambda ,\mu }$$\end{document}M≅kλ,μ for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda , \mu \in \Bbbk $$\end{document}λ,μ∈k. If M is a one-dimensional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b-representation, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}\cdot M = 0$$\end{document}P·M=0, so M can be viewed as a representation over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}/\mathcal {P}\cong \mathcal {O}$$\end{document}b/P≅O. □ □

Whittaker modules

Having defined and analysed the filtrations for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b, we now study Whittaker modules for these Lie algebras (recall Lemma 2.11). We emphasise that the construction of the filtration from Sect. 5 is not compatible with the known central extension of the BCCA, so we focus on the centreless BCCA in this section.

Whittaker modules for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O

We start by defining Whittaker functions for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O, which in turn define universal Whittaker modules for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O. We further characterise precisely when these modules are irreducible.

Definition 6.1

A Whittaker function on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O is a Lie algebra homomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n :\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}\rightarrow \Bbbk $$\end{document}φn:FnO→k for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N.

As we show next, a Whittaker function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n :\mathcal {F}_n \mathcal {O}\rightarrow \Bbbk $$\end{document}φn:FnO→k is determined by its values on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_n, \dots , \mathcal {U}_{2n+2}$$\end{document}Un,⋯,U2n+2.

Lemma 6.2

A Whittaker function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n :\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}\rightarrow \Bbbk $$\end{document}φn:FnO→k is determined by its values on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_n, \dots , \mathcal {U}_{2n+2}$$\end{document}Un,⋯,U2n+2, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{m + 2}) = 4\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_m)$$\end{document}φn(Um+2)=4φn(Um) for all m ≥ 2n + 1m≥2n+1.

Proof

Follows immediately from Lemma 5.4 and Proposition 5.7. □ □

Definition 6.3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n :\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}\rightarrow \Bbbk $$\end{document}φn:FnO→k be a Whittaker function and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk _{\varphi _n} = \Bbbk 1_{\varphi _n}$$\end{document}kφn=k1φn be the one-dimensional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}FnO-module defined by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n$$\end{document}φn, in other words,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} x \cdot 1_{\varphi _n} = \varphi _n(x)1_{\varphi _n} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}x·1φn=φn(x)1φnfor all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in \mathcal {F}_n\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}x∈Fnb. Define the universal Whittaker module for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O of type \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n$$\end{document}φn to be the induced module\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M_{\varphi _n} :={{\,\textrm{Ind}\,}}_{\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}}^{\mathcal {O}}\Bbbk _{\varphi _n}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Mφn:=IndFnOOkφn.

Remark 6.4

Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk _{\varphi _n}$$\end{document}kφn is a continuous module for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}FnO in the sense of Rudakov [75] if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n + 1}) = \varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n + 2}) = 0$$\end{document}φn(U2n+1)=φn(U2n+2)=0. In fact, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n + 1}) \ne 0$$\end{document}φn(U2n+1)≠0, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi (\mathcal {U}_{2n + 2k + 1}) \ne 0$$\end{document}φ(U2n+2k+1)≠0 for all k ≥ 0k≥0, and if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n + 2}) \ne 0$$\end{document}φn(U2n+2)≠0, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2m + 2k + 2}) \ne 0$$\end{document}φn(U2m+2k+2)≠0 for all k ≥ 0k≥0, by Lemma 6.2.

Remark 6.5

The Lie algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_0 \mathcal {O}$$\end{document}F0O is not quasi-nilpotent [30, Remark 3.5], so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _0}$$\end{document}Mφ0 is not a Whittaker module according to Definition 2.9. As we will see, the criteria for irreducibility for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _0}$$\end{document}Mφ0 and their proofs are slightly different to those for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _n}$$\end{document}Mφn with n ≥ 1n≥1, highlighting the importance of the quasi-nilpotence condition. However, to avoid piling onto the nomenclature, we abuse terminology and opt to call \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _0}$$\end{document}Mφ0 a universal Whittaker module.

We also highlight that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n \mathcal {O}$$\end{document}FnO is not an ideal of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O if n ≥ 0n≥0. Therefore, the universal Whittaker modules \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _n}$$\end{document}Mφn are never quasi-Whittaker modules according to Definition 2.12.

The following result is the main theorem of this subsection.

Theorem 6.6

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n :\mathcal {F}_n \mathcal {O}\rightarrow \Bbbk $$\end{document}φn:FnO→k be a Whittaker function. If n = 0n=0, the universal Whittaker module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _0}$$\end{document}Mφ0 is irreducible if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _0(\mathcal {U}_{2}) \ne 4 \varphi _0(\mathcal {U}_{0})$$\end{document}φ0(U2)≠4φ0(U0). If n ≥ 1n≥1, the universal Whittaker module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _n}$$\end{document}Mφn is irreducible if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n + 1}) \ne 4 \varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n - 1})$$\end{document}φn(U2n+1)≠4φn(U2n-1) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n + 2}) \ne 4\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n})$$\end{document}φn(U2n+2)≠4φn(U2n).

We now proceed to prove that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _n}$$\end{document}Mφn is reducible if the condition from Theorem 6.6 is not satisfied, proving one direction of Theorem 6.6. As mentioned in Remark 6.5, the proofs are slightly different depending on whether n = 0n=0 or n ≥ 1n≥1.

Lemma 6.7

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _0 :\mathcal {F}_0 \mathcal {O}\rightarrow \Bbbk $$\end{document}φ0:F0O→k be a Whittaker function with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _0(\mathcal {U}_{2}) = 4\varphi _0(\mathcal {U}_{0})$$\end{document}φ0(U2)=4φ0(U0). Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(\mathcal {O})\mathcal {U}_{-1} \cdot 1_{\varphi _0}$$\end{document}U(O)U-1·1φ0 is a proper submodule of the universal Whittaker module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _0}$$\end{document}Mφ0.

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i :=\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{i})$$\end{document}λi:=φn(Ui) for i ≥ 0i≥0, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w :=(\lambda _1 - 4 \, \mathcal {U}_{-1}) \cdot 1_{\varphi _0}=[\mathcal {U}_0, \mathcal {U}_{-1}]\cdot 1_{\varphi _0}$$\end{document}w:=(λ1-4U-1)·1φ0=[U0,U-1]·1φ0. We claim that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(\mathcal {O}) \cdot w$$\end{document}U(O)·w is a proper \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-submodule of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _0}$$\end{document}Mφ0.

Lemma 6.2 together with the assumption that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _2 = 4\lambda _0$$\end{document}λ2=4λ0 imply that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{n + 2} = 4\lambda _n$$\end{document}λn+2=4λn for all n ≥ 0n≥0. Then,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {U}_0 \cdot w&= \mathcal {U}_0(\lambda _1 - 4 \, \mathcal {U}_{-1}) \cdot 1_{\varphi _0} \\&= \Big (\lambda _0\lambda _1 - 4[\mathcal {U}_0,\mathcal {U}_{-1}] - 4 \, \mathcal {U}_{-1}\mathcal {U}_0\Big ) \cdot 1_{\varphi _0} \\&= \Big (\lambda _0\lambda _1 - 4(\mathcal {U}_1 - 4 \, \mathcal {U}_{-1}) - 4 \lambda _0 \, \mathcal {U}_{-1}\Big ) \cdot 1_{\varphi _0} \\&= \Big ((\lambda _0 - 4)\lambda _1 - 4(\lambda _0 - 4)\mathcal {U}_{-1}\Big ) \cdot 1_{\varphi _0} \\&= (\lambda _0 - 4)w. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}U0·w=U0(λ1-4U-1)·1φ0=(λ0λ1-4[U0,U-1]-4U-1U0)·1φ0=(λ0λ1-4(U1-4U-1)-4λ0U-1)·1φ0=((λ0-4)λ1-4(λ0-4)U-1)·1φ0=(λ0-4)w.Now letting n ≥ 1n≥1, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {U}_n \cdot w&= \mathcal {U}_n(\lambda _1 - 4\,\mathcal {U}_{-1}) \cdot 1_{\varphi _0} \\&= \Big (\lambda _n\lambda _1 - 4[\mathcal {U}_n,\mathcal {U}_{-1}] - 4\,\mathcal {U}_{-1}\mathcal {U}_n\Big ) \cdot 1_{\varphi _0} \\&= \Big (\lambda _n\lambda _1 - 4(n + 1)(\mathcal {U}_{n + 1} - 4\,\mathcal {U}_{n - 1}) - 4\lambda _n \, \mathcal {U}_{-1}\Big ) \cdot 1_{\varphi _0} \\&= \lambda _n (\lambda _1 - 4\,\mathcal {U}_{-1}) \cdot 1_{\varphi _0} - 4(n + 1)(\lambda _{n + 1} - 4\lambda _{n - 1}) \cdot 1_{\varphi _0} \\&= \lambda _n w, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Un·w=Un(λ1-4U-1)·1φ0=(λnλ1-4[Un,U-1]-4U-1Un)·1φ0=(λnλ1-4(n+1)(Un+1-4Un-1)-4λnU-1)·1φ0=λn(λ1-4U-1)·1φ0-4(n+1)(λn+1-4λn-1)·1φ0=λnw,where we used that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{n + 1} = 4\lambda _{n - 1}$$\end{document}λn+1=4λn-1 in the last equality, since n ≥ 1n≥1. This proves the claim, and the result follows. □ □

Remark 6.8

When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _2 = 4\lambda _0$$\end{document}λ2=4λ0, the submodule \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(\mathcal {O})(\lambda _1 - 4 \, \mathcal {U}_{-1}) \cdot 1_{\varphi _0}$$\end{document}U(O)(λ1-4U-1)·1φ0 in the proof of Lemma 6.7 is isomorphic to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _0'}$$\end{document}Mφ0′, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi '_0 :\mathcal {F}_0\mathcal {O}\rightarrow \Bbbk $$\end{document}φ0′:F0O→k is the Whittaker function with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi '_0(\mathcal {U}_0) = \lambda _0 - 4$$\end{document}φ0′(U0)=λ0-4 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi '_0(\mathcal {U}_n) = \lambda _n$$\end{document}φ0′(Un)=λn for n ≥ 1n≥1.

In this case, the quotient module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _0}/(U(\mathcal {O})(\lambda _1 - 4 \, \mathcal {U}_{-1}) \cdot 1_{\varphi _0})$$\end{document}Mφ0/(U(O)(λ1-4U-1)·1φ0) is a 1-dimensional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-module determined by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_{-1} \cdot 1_{\varphi _0} = \frac{\lambda _1}{4} 1_{\varphi _0}, \mathcal {U}_{0} \cdot 1_{\varphi _0} = \lambda _0 1_{\varphi _0}$$\end{document}U-1·1φ0=λ141φ0,U0·1φ0=λ01φ0.

The case n ≥ 1n≥1 follows from the existence of an analogous submodule.

Lemma 6.9

Let n ≥ 1n≥1 and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n :\mathcal {F}_n \mathcal {O}\rightarrow \Bbbk $$\end{document}φn:FnO→k be a Whittaker function with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n + 1}) = 4 \varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n - 1})$$\end{document}φn(U2n+1)=4φn(U2n-1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n + 2}) = 4\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{2n})$$\end{document}φn(U2n+2)=4φn(U2n). Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(\mathcal {O}) \mathcal {U}_{n - 1} \cdot 1_{\varphi _n}$$\end{document}U(O)Un-1·1φn is a submodule of the universal Whittaker module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _n}$$\end{document}Mφn.

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i :=\varphi _n(\mathcal {U}_{i})$$\end{document}λi:=φn(Ui) for i ≥ ni≥n, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w :=\mathcal {U}_{n - 1} \cdot 1_{\varphi _n}$$\end{document}w:=Un-1·1φn. We claim that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(\mathcal {O}) \cdot w$$\end{document}U(O)·w is a proper submodule of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _n}$$\end{document}Mφn.

Lemma 6.2 together with the assumptions that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{2n + 1} = 4\lambda _{2n - 1}$$\end{document}λ2n+1=4λ2n-1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{2n + 2} = 4\lambda _{2n}$$\end{document}λ2n+2=4λ2n imply that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{k + 2} = 4\lambda _k$$\end{document}λk+2=4λk for all k ≥ 2n - 1k≥2n-1. For m ≥ nm≥n, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {U}_m \cdot w&= \mathcal {U}_m \mathcal {U}_{n - 1} \cdot 1_{\psi _n} = [\mathcal {U}_m,\mathcal {U}_{n - 1}] \cdot 1_{\psi _n} + \mathcal {U}_{n - 1} \mathcal {U}_m \cdot 1_{\psi _n} \\&= (m - n + 1) (\mathcal {U}_{m + n + 1} - 4 \, \mathcal {U}_{m + n - 1}) \cdot 1_{\psi _n} + \lambda _m w \\&= \lambda _m w, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Um·w=UmUn-1·1ψn=[Um,Un-1]·1ψn+Un-1Um·1ψn=(m-n+1)(Um+n+1-4Um+n-1)·1ψn+λmw=λmw,since m + n - 1 ≥ 2n - 1m+n-1≥2n-1, where we used that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{k + 2} = 4\lambda _k$$\end{document}λk+2=4λk for all k ≥ 2n - 1k≥2n-1.

The above shows that 𝕜 wkw is a one-dimensional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n \mathcal {O}$$\end{document}FnO-module isomorphic to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk _{\varphi _n}$$\end{document}kφn. It is now easy to see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(\mathcal {O}) \cdot w$$\end{document}U(O)·w is a proper submodule of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _n}$$\end{document}Mφn isomorphic to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _n}$$\end{document}Mφn itself. □ □

Note that together Lemmas 6.7 and 6.9 prove one direction of Theorem 6.6. To prove the converse, we use the theory of local functions and the orbit method, which we introduce in the next subsection.

Local functions and the orbit method for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O

In this subsection, we make a key observation that the Whittaker function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _n$$\end{document}φn from Theorem 6.6 can be viewed as the restriction of a certain element χ χ of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_1^*$$\end{document}W1∗ whose stabilizer has finite codimension in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}W1. Then Whittaker modules are exactly restrictions of modules from the Kirillov orbit method for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}W1 (see [71, 81]). Kirillov’s orbit method [58] is a guiding principle in Lie theory, establishing a correspondence between equivalence classes of unitary irreducible representations and coadjoint orbits. As a result, we will readily obtain the irreducibility criteria in Theorem 6.6.

Note that in this subsection, we view \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_1 = {{\,\textrm{Der}\,}}(\Bbbk [s]) = \Bbbk [s]\partial _s$$\end{document}W1=Der(k[s])=k[s]∂s (in other words, we use the variable s instead of t), so that we can view \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O as the subalgebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(s^2 - 4)\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}(s2-4)W1 of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}W1. We start by recalling the notion of a local function on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_{1}$$\end{document}W1, originally defined in [72].

Definition 6.10

Letting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x, \alpha _0, \alpha _1, \dots ,\alpha _n \in \Bbbk $$\end{document}x,α0,α1,⋯,αn∈k with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _n \ne 0$$\end{document}αn≠0, we define a one-point local function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _{x; \alpha _0, \dots ,\alpha _n} \in \mathbb {W}_1^*$$\end{document}χx;α0,⋯,αn∈W1∗ by6.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \chi _{x; \alpha _0, \dots ,\alpha _n} :\mathbb {W}_{1} \rightarrow \Bbbk , \quad f \partial \mapsto \alpha _0 f(x) + \alpha _1 f'(x) + \dots +\alpha _n f^{(n)}(x), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χx;α0,⋯,αn:W1→k,f∂↦α0f(x)+α1f′(x)+⋯+αnf(n)(x),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in \Bbbk [s]$$\end{document}f∈k[s] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^{(i)}$$\end{document}f(i) denotes taking the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i^{\text {th}}$$\end{document}ith derivative of f with respect to s. We call x the base point of χ χ and n the order of χ χ.

A local function χ χ on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}W1 is a sum of finitely many functions of the form (6.1) with (possibly) distinct x. In other words,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \chi = \chi _1 + \dots + \chi _{\ell }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χ=χ1+⋯+χℓ,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _i$$\end{document}χi is a one-point local function based at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_i \in \Bbbk $$\end{document}xi∈k of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_i$$\end{document}ni, and the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_i$$\end{document}xi are pairwise distinct.

Next, we recall the notion of a polarisation for a local function on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_{1}$$\end{document}W1 in [71].

Definition 6.11

For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi \in \mathbb {W}_{1}^*$$\end{document}χ∈W1∗, we define a bilinear form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} B_\chi : \mathbb {W}_{1} \times \mathbb {W}_{1}&\rightarrow \Bbbk \\ (u, v)&\mapsto \chi ([u, v]). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Bχ:W1×W1→k(u,v)↦χ([u,v]).A polarisation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {p}_\chi $$\end{document}pχ of χ χ is a finite-codimensional subalgebra of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_{1}$$\end{document}W1 that is maximally totally isotropic subspace with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_\chi $$\end{document}Bχ, in other words, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_\chi ([\mathfrak {p}_\chi , \mathfrak {p}_\chi ]) = 0$$\end{document}Bχ([pχ,pχ])=0.

Remark 6.12

In general, an arbitrary element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi \in \mathfrak {g}^*$$\end{document}χ∈g∗ for a Lie algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {g}$$\end{document}g might not have a polarisation. However, if χ χ is a local function on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}W1, then χ χ always has a polarisation [71, Proposition 3.2]. In this case, the polarisation can moreover be chosen as a submodule-subalgebra of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}W1.

We consider an example of a local function and its polarisation to illustrate the concept.

Example 6.13

([72]) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi :=\chi _{x; \nu _0, \nu _1}$$\end{document}χ:=χx;ν0,ν1 be a one-point local function on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}W1 where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in \Bbbk $$\end{document}x∈k and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\nu _0, \nu _1) \in \Bbbk ^2 \setminus \{(0, 0)\}$$\end{document}(ν0,ν1)∈k2\{(0,0)}. In other words,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \chi (f\partial ) = \nu _0 f(x) + \nu _1 f'(x). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χ(f∂)=ν0f(x)+ν1f′(x).Consider the submodule-subalgebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(s - x)\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}(s-x)W1 of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}W1. Certainly, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(s - x)\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}(s-x)W1 has codimension 1 in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}W1. Moreover, (4.1) implies that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \chi ([(s - x)\mathbb {W}_1, (s - x)\mathbb {W}_1]) = \chi ((s - x)^2\mathbb {W}_1) = 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χ([(s-x)W1,(s-x)W1])=χ((s-x)2W1)=0,and therefore \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(s - x)\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}(s-x)W1 is a totally isotropic subspace of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}W1 with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_\chi $$\end{document}Bχ. Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(s - x)\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}(s-x)W1 is maximally totally isotropic by a dimension count [71, Lemma 3.1], and therefore is a polarisation of χ χ.

Remark 6.14

The polarisation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(s - x)\mathbb {W}_1$$\end{document}(s-x)W1 is in fact the unique polarisation for the local function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _{x; \nu _0, \nu _1}$$\end{document}χx;ν0,ν1 (see, for example, [72, Section 7.1] for a proof). However, polarisations for local functions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}_{1}$$\end{document}W1 are in general not unique (for an example, see [71, Example 3.3]).

Remark 6.15
