Recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}= {{\,\textrm{span}\,}}\{\mathcal {V}_k| k \ge -1 \}$$\end{document}P=span{Vk|k≥-1} is an abelian subalgebra of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b.

Lemma 6.33

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x = \mathcal {U}_{r_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{r_\ell } v \in M_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}x=Ur1⋯Urℓv∈Mψn, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v \in U(\mathcal {P}) \cdot 1_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}v∈U(P)·1ψn and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le r_1 \le r_2 \le \dots \le r_\ell \le n - 1$$\end{document}0≤r1≤r2≤⋯≤rℓ≤n-1. Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\kappa + m} \cdot x = 0$$\end{document}Vκ+m·x=0 for all m ≥ 1m≥1.

Proof

The result is clearly true if n = 0n=0, so assume that n ≥ 1n≥1. We proceed by induction on ℓ ℓ, the base case ℓ = 0ℓ=0 being trivial. For the induction step, assume that the statement holds for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell \in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}ℓ∈N. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x = \mathcal {U}_{r_0} \mathcal {U}_{r_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{r_\ell } v$$\end{document}x=Ur0Ur1⋯Urℓv, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v \in U(\mathcal {P}) \cdot 1_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}v∈U(P)·1ψn and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le r_0 \le r_1 \le \dots \le r_\ell \le n - 1$$\end{document}0≤r0≤r1≤⋯≤rℓ≤n-1. For all m ≥ 1m≥1, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {V}_{\kappa + m} \cdot x&= \mathcal {V}_{\kappa + m} \mathcal {U}_{r_0} \mathcal {U}_{r_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{r_\ell } v \\&= [\mathcal {V}_{\kappa + m},\mathcal {U}_{r_0}]\mathcal {U}_{r_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{r_\ell } v + \mathcal {U}_{r_0} \mathcal {V}_{\kappa + m} \mathcal {U}_{r_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{r_\ell } v \\&= \Big ((\kappa + m - r_0 - 1)\mathcal {V}_{\kappa + m + r_0 + 2} - 4(\kappa + m - r_0)\mathcal {V}_{\kappa + m + r_0}\Big )\mathcal {U}_{r_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{r_\ell } v \\&= 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Vκ+m·x=Vκ+mUr0Ur1⋯Urℓv=[Vκ+m,Ur0]Ur1⋯Urℓv+Ur0Vκ+mUr1⋯Urℓv=((κ+m-r0-1)Vκ+m+r0+2-4(κ+m-r0)Vκ+m+r0)Ur1⋯Urℓv=0,where we used the induction hypothesis in the last two equalities. □ □

Next, we extend Lemma 6.33 to the situation where the element of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}Mψn contains \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_{-1}$$\end{document}U-1.

Lemma 6.34

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in U(\mathcal {F}_0 \mathcal {O}\ltimes \mathcal {P}) \cdot 1_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}x∈U(F0O⋉P)·1ψn. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\kappa + m} \cdot \mathcal {U}_{-1}^N x = 0$$\end{document}Vκ+m·U-1Nx=0 if m > Nm>N.

Proof

Follows via an easy induction on N, with Lemma 6.33 as the base case. □ □

In the following result, we show that the action \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\kappa + N} \cdot \mathcal {U}_{-1}^N x$$\end{document}Vκ+N·U-1Nx results in the complete removal of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_{-1}$$\end{document}U-1, in other words, that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\kappa + N} \cdot \mathcal {U}_{-1}^N x$$\end{document}Vκ+N·U-1Nx is a nonzero element of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(\mathcal {F}_0 \mathcal {O}\ltimes \mathcal {P}) \cdot 1_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}U(F0O⋉P)·1ψn, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in U(\mathcal {F}_0 \mathcal {O}\ltimes \mathcal {P}) \cdot 1_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}x∈U(F0O⋉P)·1ψn.

Proposition 6.35

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in U(\mathcal {F}_0 \mathcal {O}\ltimes \mathcal {P}) \cdot 1_{\psi _n} \setminus \{0\}$$\end{document}x∈U(F0O⋉P)·1ψn\{0}. Then, for any N ≥ 1N≥1, there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c \in \Bbbk \setminus \{0\}$$\end{document}c∈k\{0} such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {V}_{\kappa + N} \cdot \mathcal {U}_{-1}^N x = c x + {\text {lower degree terms in }} U(\mathcal {F}_0 \mathcal {O}\ltimes \mathcal {P}) \ne 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Vκ+N·U-1Nx=cx+lower degree terms inU(F0O⋉P)≠0,where lower degree terms means terms with lower degree than x under the PBW filtration on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(\mathfrak {b})$$\end{document}U(b).

Proof

We proceed by induction on N. First, consider the base case N = 0N=0:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {V}_\kappa \cdot x = \mu _\kappa x + {\text {lower degree terms in }} U(\mathcal {F}_0 \mathcal {O}\ltimes \mathcal {P}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Vκ·x=μκx+lower degree terms inU(F0O⋉P),which is nonzero.

For the induction step, assume the statement is true for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}N∈N. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {V}_{\kappa + N + 1} \cdot \mathcal {U}_{-1}^{N + 1} x&= [\mathcal {V}_{\kappa + N + 1}, \mathcal {U}_{-1}] \mathcal {U}_{-1}^N x + \mathcal {U}_{-1} \mathcal {V}_{\kappa + N + 1} \mathcal {U}_{-1}^N x \\&= \Big ((\kappa + N + 1)\mathcal {V}_{\kappa + N + 2} - 4(\kappa + N + 2)\mathcal {V}_{\kappa + N}\Big ) \mathcal {U}_{-1}^N x \\&= -4(\kappa + N + 2)\mathcal {V}_{\kappa + N} \mathcal {U}_{-1}^N x, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Vκ+N+1·U-1N+1x=[Vκ+N+1,U-1]U-1Nx+U-1Vκ+N+1U-1Nx=((κ+N+1)Vκ+N+2-4(κ+N+2)Vκ+N)U-1Nx=-4(κ+N+2)Vκ+NU-1Nx,where we used Lemma 6.34 in the last two equalities. By the induction hypothesis, it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\kappa + N + 1} \cdot \mathcal {U}_{-1}^{N + 1} x \in U(\mathcal {F}_0 \mathcal {O}\ltimes \mathcal {P}) \setminus \{0\}$$\end{document}Vκ+N+1·U-1N+1x∈U(F0O⋉P)\{0}, which concludes the proof. □ □

Having shown how \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_{-1}$$\end{document}U-1 can be removed from any element of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(\mathfrak {b})$$\end{document}U(b), we now proceed to also remove \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_0$$\end{document}U0.

Proposition 6.36

Suppose n ≥ 1n≥1. Then the following hold. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in U(\mathcal {F}_1 \mathcal {O}\ltimes \mathcal {P}) \cdot 1_{\psi _n} \setminus \{0\}$$\end{document}x∈U(F1O⋉P)·1ψn\{0}. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_\kappa \cdot x = \mu _\kappa x$$\end{document}Vκ·x=μκx.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in U(\mathcal {F}_1 \mathcal {O}\ltimes \mathcal {P}) \cdot 1_{\psi _n} \setminus \{0\}$$\end{document}x∈U(F1O⋉P)·1ψn\{0}. Then there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c \in \Bbbk \setminus \{0\}$$\end{document}c∈k\{0} such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {V}_{\kappa }-\mu _\kappa )^N \cdot \mathcal {U}_{0}^N x = cx$$\end{document}(Vκ-μκ)N·U0Nx=cx.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in U(\mathcal {F}_1 \mathcal {O}\ltimes \mathcal {P}) \cdot 1_{\psi _n} \setminus \{0\}$$\end{document}x∈U(F1O⋉P)·1ψn\{0}. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_\kappa \cdot x = \mu _\kappa x$$\end{document}Vκ·x=μκx.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in U(\mathcal {F}_1 \mathcal {O}\ltimes \mathcal {P}) \cdot 1_{\psi _n} \setminus \{0\}$$\end{document}x∈U(F1O⋉P)·1ψn\{0}. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_\kappa \cdot x = \mu _\kappa x$$\end{document}Vκ·x=μκx.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in U(\mathcal {F}_1 \mathcal {O}\ltimes \mathcal {P}) \cdot 1_{\psi _n} \setminus \{0\}$$\end{document}x∈U(F1O⋉P)·1ψn\{0}. Then there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c \in \Bbbk \setminus \{0\}$$\end{document}c∈k\{0} such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {V}_{\kappa }-\mu _\kappa )^N \cdot \mathcal {U}_{0}^N x = cx$$\end{document}(Vκ-μκ)N·U0Nx=cx.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in U(\mathcal {F}_1 \mathcal {O}\ltimes \mathcal {P}) \cdot 1_{\psi _n} \setminus \{0\}$$\end{document}x∈U(F1O⋉P)·1ψn\{0}. Then there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c \in \Bbbk \setminus \{0\}$$\end{document}c∈k\{0} such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {V}_{\kappa }-\mu _\kappa )^N \cdot \mathcal {U}_{0}^N x = cx$$\end{document}(Vκ-μκ)N·U0Nx=cx.

Proof

For (1), it suffices to consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x = \mathcal {U}_{r_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{r_\ell }y$$\end{document}x=Ur1⋯Urℓy, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y \in U(\mathcal {P}) \cdot 1_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}y∈U(P)·1ψn and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le r_1 \le r_2 \le \dots \le r_\ell \le n - 1$$\end{document}1≤r1≤r2≤⋯≤rℓ≤n-1. The result is clearly true for n = 1n=1, so assume that n ≥ 2n≥2. We proceed by induction on ℓ ℓ, the case ℓ = 0ℓ=0 being trivial. For the induction step, assume that the statement is true for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell \in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}ℓ∈N. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {V}_\kappa \cdot \mathcal {U}_{r_0} \mathcal {U}_{r_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{r_\ell } y = \mathcal {U}_{r_0} \mathcal {V}_\kappa \mathcal {U}_{r_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{r_\ell } y + [\mathcal {V}_\kappa ,\mathcal {U}_{r_0}]\mathcal {U}_{r_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{r_\ell } y. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Vκ·Ur0Ur1⋯Urℓy=Ur0VκUr1⋯Urℓy+[Vκ,Ur0]Ur1⋯Urℓy.By the induction hypothesis, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {V}_\kappa \mathcal {U}_{r_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{r_\ell } y = \mu _\kappa \mathcal {V}_\kappa \mathcal {U}_{r_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{r_\ell } y. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}VκUr1⋯Urℓy=μκVκUr1⋯Urℓy.Therefore,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {V}_\kappa \cdot \mathcal {U}_{r_0} \mathcal {U}_{r_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{r_\ell } y = \mu _\kappa \mathcal {U}_{r_0} \mathcal {U}_{r_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{r_\ell } y + \Big ((\kappa - r_0 - 1)\mathcal {V}_{\kappa + r_0 + 2} - 4(\kappa - r_0)\mathcal {V}_{\kappa + r_0}\Big )\mathcal {U}_{r_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{r_\ell } y. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Vκ·Ur0Ur1⋯Urℓy=μκUr0Ur1⋯Urℓy+((κ-r0-1)Vκ+r0+2-4(κ-r0)Vκ+r0)Ur1⋯Urℓy.By Lemma 6.33, it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Big ((\kappa - r_0 - 1)\mathcal {V}_{\kappa + r_0 + 2} - 4(\kappa - r_0)\mathcal {V}_{\kappa + r_0}\Big )\mathcal {U}_{r_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{r_\ell } y = 0$$\end{document}((κ-r0-1)Vκ+r0+2-4(κ-r0)Vκ+r0)Ur1⋯Urℓy=0, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_0 \ge 1$$\end{document}r0≥1. We conclude that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_\kappa \cdot \mathcal {U}_{r_0} \mathcal {U}_{r_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{r_\ell } y = \mu _\kappa \mathcal {U}_{r_0} \mathcal {U}_{r_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{r_\ell } y$$\end{document}Vκ·Ur0Ur1⋯Urℓy=μκUr0Ur1⋯Urℓy, which concludes the proof of (1).

For (2), the proof is similar to that of Proposition 6.35, using (1). □ □

The next result shows how to remove all the other \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_i$$\end{document}Ui from an element of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(\mathfrak {b})$$\end{document}U(b) by successively acting by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {V}_m - \mu _m)$$\end{document}(Vm-μm) for some appropriately chosen \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m \in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}m∈N. Of course, if n = 0n=0 or n = 1n=1, then we have already removed all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_i$$\end{document}Ui in the previous results, so we do not need to consider these cases.

Proposition 6.37

Suppose n ≥ 2n≥2, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x = \mathcal {U}_{q_1}^{p_{1}} \dots \mathcal {U}_{q_k}^{p_{k}} y$$\end{document}x=Uq11⋯Uqkky, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y \in U(\mathcal {P}) \cdot 1_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}y∈U(P)·1ψn and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le q_1< q_2< \dots < q_k \le n - 1$$\end{document}1≤q1<q2<⋯<qk≤n-1. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {V}_m - \mu _m) \cdot x = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} -4 p_1 \mu _\kappa (\kappa - 2q_1) \mathcal {U}_{q_1}^{p_{1} - 1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{q_k}^{p_{k}} y, & {\text {if }} m = \kappa - q_1, \\ 0, & {\text {if }} m > \kappa - q_1. \end{array}\right. }$$\end{document}(Vm-μm)·x=-4p1μκ(κ-2q1)Uq1p1-1⋯Uqkky,ifm=κ-q1,0,ifm>κ-q1.

Proof

Rewrite x as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} x = \mathcal {U}_{s_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{s_\ell } y, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}x=Us1⋯Usℓy,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le s_1 \le s_2 \le \dots \le s_\ell \le n - 1$$\end{document}1≤s1≤s2≤⋯≤sℓ≤n-1. Of course, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_1 = q_1$$\end{document}s1=q1. For m ≥ nm≥n, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {V}_m \cdot x&= \mathcal {V}_m \mathcal {U}_{s_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{s_\ell } y \\&= \mathcal {U}_{s_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{s_\ell } \mathcal {V}_m y + [\mathcal {V}_m,\mathcal {U}_{s_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{s_\ell }]y \\&= \mu _m x + \sum _{j = 1}^\ell \mathcal {U}_{s_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{s_{j - 1}} [\mathcal {V}_m,\mathcal {U}_{s_j}] \mathcal {U}_{s_{j + 1}} \dots \mathcal {U}_{s_\ell }y \\&= \mu _m x + \sum _{j = 1}^\ell \mathcal {U}_{s_1} \dots \mathcal {U}_{s_{j - 1}} \Big ((m - s_j - 1)\mathcal {V}_{m + s_j + 2} - 4(m - s_j)\mathcal {V}_{m + s_j}\Big ) \mathcal {U}_{s_{j + 1}} \dots \mathcal {U}_{s_\ell }y. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Vm·x=VmUs1⋯Usℓy=Us1⋯UsℓVmy+[Vm,Us1⋯Usℓ]y=μmx+∑j=1ℓUs1⋯Usj-1[Vm,Usj]Usj+1⋯Usℓy=μmx+∑j=1ℓUs1⋯Usj-1((m-sj-1)Vm+sj+2-4(m-sj)Vm+sj)Usj+1⋯Usℓy.If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m + s_j > \kappa $$\end{document}m+sj>κ, then the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j^{\text {th}}$$\end{document}jth term of the above summation is zero, by Lemma 6.33. In particular, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m > \kappa - s_1 = \kappa - q_1$$\end{document}m>κ-s1=κ-q1, then the entire summation vanishes, and thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_m \cdot x = \mu _m x$$\end{document}Vm·x=μmx, as required.

Now suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m = \kappa - s_1 = \kappa - q_1$$\end{document}m=κ-s1=κ-q1. By the above discussion, we can only get a contribution from the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j^{\text {th}}$$\end{document}jth term of the above summation if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_j = s_1$$\end{document}sj=s1, since in this case we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m + s_j = \kappa $$\end{document}m+sj=κ. Therefore, switching back to the original notation for x, we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {V}_{\kappa - q_1} \cdot x = \mu _{\kappa - q_1} x - 4(\kappa - 2q_1) \sum _{i = 0}^{p_1 - 1} \mathcal {U}_{q_1}^i \mathcal {V}_\kappa \mathcal {U}_{q_1}^{p_1 - i - 1} \mathcal {U}_{q_2}^{p_2} \dots \mathcal {U}_{q_k}^{p_k} y. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Vκ-q1·x=μκ-q1x-4(κ-2q1)∑i=0p1-1Uq1iVκUq1p1-i-1Uq22⋯Uqkky.By Proposition 6.36(1), it follows that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {V}_\kappa \cdot \mathcal {U}_{q_1}^{p_1 - i - 1} \mathcal {U}_{q_2}^{p_2} \dots \mathcal {U}_{q_k}^{p_k} y = \mu _\kappa \mathcal {U}_{q_1}^{p_1 - i - 1} \mathcal {U}_{q_2}^{p_2} \dots \mathcal {U}_{q_k}^{p_k} y, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Vκ·Uq1p1-i-1Uq22⋯Uqkky=μκUq1p1-i-1Uq22⋯Uqkky,and thus\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {V}_{\kappa - q_1} \cdot x = \mu _{\kappa - q_1} x - 4 p_1 \mu _\kappa (\kappa - 2q_1) \mathcal {U}_{q_1}^{p_1 - 1} \mathcal {U}_{q_2}^{p_2} \dots \mathcal {U}_{q_k}^{p_k} y, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Vκ-q1·x=μκ-q1x-4p1μκ(κ-2q1)Uq1p1-1Uq22⋯Uqkky,which concludes the proof. □ □

The next result is a direct consequence of Propositions 6.35, 6.36, and 6.37.

Corollary 6.38

Let M be a nonzero submodule of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}Mψn. Then, M contains a nonzero element in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(\mathcal {P}) \cdot 1_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}U(P)·1ψn. □ □

Thanks to Corollary 6.38, we can simply focus on elements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}Mψn not containing any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_i$$\end{document}Ui. Thankfully, the removal of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_j$$\end{document}Vj is much easier, since the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_j$$\end{document}Vj commute with each other.

Lemma 6.39

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y = \mathcal {V}_{q_1}^{p_1} \dots \mathcal {V}_{q_k}^{p_k} \cdot 1_{\psi _n} \in M_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}y=Vq11⋯Vqkk·1ψn∈Mψn with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-1 \le q_1< \dots < q_k \le n - 1$$\end{document}-1≤q1<⋯<qk≤n-1. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {U}_m - \lambda _m) \cdot y = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} -4 p_1 \mu _\kappa (\kappa - 2q_1) \mathcal {V}_{q_1}^{p_1 - 1} \mathcal {V}_{q_2}^{p_2} \dots \mathcal {V}_{q_k}^{p_k} \cdot 1_{\psi _n}, & {\text {if }} m = \kappa - q_1, \\ 0, & {\text {if }} m > \kappa - q_1. \end{array}\right. }.$$\end{document}(Um-λm)·y=-4p1μκ(κ-2q1)Vq1p1-1Vq22⋯Vqkk·1ψn,ifm=κ-q1,0,ifm>κ-q1..

Proof

Rewrite y as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} y = \mathcal {V}_{s_1} \dots \mathcal {V}_{s_\ell } \cdot 1_{\psi _n}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}y=Vs1⋯Vsℓ·1ψn,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-1 \le s_1 \le s_2 \le \dots \le s_\ell \le n - 1$$\end{document}-1≤s1≤s2≤⋯≤sℓ≤n-1 (and thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_1 = q_1$$\end{document}s1=q1). We have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {U}_m \cdot y&= \mathcal {U}_m \mathcal {V}_{s_1} \dots \mathcal {V}_{s_\ell } \cdot 1_{\psi _n} = \mathcal {V}_{s_1} \dots \mathcal {V}_{s_\ell } \mathcal {U}_m \cdot 1_{\psi _n} + \sum _{j = 1}^\ell \mathcal {V}_{s_1} \dots \mathcal {V}_{s_{j - 1}} [\mathcal {U}_m,\mathcal {V}_{s_j}] \mathcal {V}_{s_{j + 1}} \dots \mathcal {V}_{s_\ell } \cdot 1_{\psi _n} \\&= \lambda _m y + \sum _{j = 1}^\ell \mathcal {V}_{s_1} \dots \mathcal {V}_{s_{j - 1}} \Big ((m - s_j + 1)\mathcal {V}_{m + s_j + 2} - 4(m - s_j)\mathcal {V}_{m + s_j}\Big ) \mathcal {V}_{s_{j + 1}} \dots \mathcal {V}_{s_\ell } \cdot 1_{\psi _n} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Um·y=UmVs1⋯Vsℓ·1ψn=Vs1⋯VsℓUm·1ψn+∑j=1ℓVs1⋯Vsj-1[Um,Vsj]Vsj+1⋯Vsℓ·1ψn=λmy+∑j=1ℓVs1⋯Vsj-1((m-sj+1)Vm+sj+2-4(m-sj)Vm+sj)Vsj+1⋯Vsℓ·1ψnSuppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m = \kappa - q_1 = \kappa - s_1$$\end{document}m=κ-q1=κ-s1. Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m + s_j > \kappa $$\end{document}m+sj>κ if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_j > s_1$$\end{document}sj>s1. Therefore, we only get a contribution from the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j^{\text {th}}$$\end{document}jth term in the above summation if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_j = s_1$$\end{document}sj=s1, since in this case we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m + s_j = \kappa $$\end{document}m+sj=κ. It follows that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {U}_{\kappa - q_1} \cdot y = \lambda _{\kappa - q_1} y - 4 p_1 \mu _\kappa (\kappa - 2q_1) \mathcal {V}_{q_1}^{p_1 - 1} \mathcal {V}_{q_2}^{p_2} \dots \mathcal {V}_{q_k}^{p_k} \cdot 1_{\psi _n}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Uκ-q1·y=λκ-q1y-4p1μκ(κ-2q1)Vq1p1-1Vq22⋯Vqkk·1ψn,which concludes the proof. The case where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m > \kappa - q_1$$\end{document}m>κ-q1 follows similarly. □ □

We are now ready to prove Theorem 6.28.

Proof of Theorem 6.28

One direction of the statement is simply Lemmas 6.29 and 6.31. For the converse, assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{2n} \ne 0$$\end{document}μ2n≠0 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{2n - 1} \ne 0$$\end{document}μ2n-1≠0, and let M be a nonzero submodule of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}Mψn. Using Corollary 6.38, choose an element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w \in M \cap (U(\mathcal {P}) \cdot 1_{\psi _n}) \setminus \{0\}$$\end{document}w∈M∩(U(P)·1ψn)\{0}. Now apply Lemma 6.39 to w repeatedly to deduce that M contains a nonzero scalar multiple of the generating vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}1ψn of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}Mψn. Therefore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M = M_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}M=Mψn, which concludes the proof. □ □

Remark 6.40

One may wonder about using the methods of [32] to prove Theorems 6.6 and 6.28. The main issue is that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n \mathcal {O}$$\end{document}FnO and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n \mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}Fnb are not ideals of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b for n ≥ 0n≥0, making it impossible to apply their results directly, since quasi-Whittaker functions must be defined on ideals of the Lie algebra (recall Definition 2.12). But \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n \mathcal {O}$$\end{document}FnO and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n \mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}Fnb are ideals of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_0 \mathcal {O}$$\end{document}F0O and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_0 \mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}F0b, so one could get some irreducibility results by inducing from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n \mathcal {O}$$\end{document}FnO or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_n \mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}Fnb to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_0 \mathcal {O}$$\end{document}F0O or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_0 \mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}F0b and analysing the Whittaker annihilator, as is done in [32]. However, deducing the irreducibility of the universal Whittaker module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi _n}$$\end{document}Mφn or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\psi _n}$$\end{document}Mψn from the irreducibility of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Ind}\,}}_{\mathcal {F}_n \mathcal {O}}^{\mathcal {F}_0 \mathcal {O}} \Bbbk _{\varphi _n}$$\end{document}IndFnOF0Okφn or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Ind}\,}}_{\mathcal {F}_n \mathfrak {b}}^{\mathcal {F}_0 \mathfrak {b}} \Bbbk _{\psi _n}$$\end{document}IndFnbF0bkψn is a non-trivial task which would require similar analysis to our proof of Theorem 6.28. For this reason, we opted for a more direct proof in the case of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b, and a proof using the orbit method for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O.

Future work

Fig. 1Diagram summarising the relations between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}$$\end{document}Vir, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathfrak {b}}$$\end{document}b^. The vertical dashed arrows denote restrictions, while the horizontal solid ones denote Lie algebra contractions

Diagram summarising the relations between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}$$\end{document}Vir, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathfrak {b}}$$\end{document}b^. The vertical dashed arrows denote restrictions, while the horizontal solid ones denote Lie algebra contractions

In summary, this paper provides new insights into the algebraic structure of the BCCA and studies some of its representations, namely restrictions of representations of larger Lie algebras and Whittaker modules. We hope our work serves as a useful starting point for further exploration of the BCCA and the related mathematical and physical concepts that one would encounter. We conclude this paper by presenting some of these future research directions: The BCCA can be obtained as the Lie algebra contraction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}$$\end{document}Vir (see [12, Equation (8)]). Hence, one could study \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b-modules obtained as contractions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}$$\end{document}Vir-modules. Contractions at the level of representations are essential in Carrollian physics [34], but there is still much that is unclear in this regard. For instance, the contractions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}\times {{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}$$\end{document}Vir×Vir-modules built from “mode algebras” or free fields (i.e. BC-systems, Heisenberg algebra, etc.) have been considered in the physics literature4 (see, for example, [54, Sections 5.1.2 and 5.2], [8, Section 4] and [9, Section 3.2.1]). However, there are still technical subtleties (e.g. issues with “normal-ordering”) when it comes to contracting these infinite-dimensional representations that need to be addressed. Attempting to construct sensible representations of the BCCA as contractions of representations of the Virasoro algebra may help address some of these subtleties (see, for instance, [74] for some work in this direction on VOA modules). They should also help guide us towards the quantisation of the tensionless open string since we expect such representations to describe the possible spectra of this string. It would be particularly interesting if representations constructed in this manner belong to one of the classes of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathfrak {b}}$$\end{document}b^-modules discussed in this paper.The above line of enquiry leads naturally to the consideration of restrictions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms-modules that are vertex operator algebra (VOA) modules [24, 41, 44] (see [84] for the first appearance of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms-modules5 in mathematics), such as the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms-module formed from two fermionic BC systems [42, 45, 48], each of conformal weight (2,-1)(2,-1). This module is also known as the semi-infinite wedge or fermionic Fock representation [42, 43], denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda ^{\boldsymbol{\cdot }}_\infty (\mathfrak {bms})$$\end{document}Λ∞·(bms). For one of the three vacua of closed tensionless string theory, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda ^{\boldsymbol{\cdot }}_\infty (\mathfrak {bms})$$\end{document}Λ∞·(bms) is the so-called ghost sector of the theory that appears during Becchi–Rouet–Stora–Tyutin (BRST) quantisation [26–28, 79]. Thus, understanding the restriction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda ^{\boldsymbol{\cdot }}_\infty (\mathfrak {bms})$$\end{document}Λ∞·(bms) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b (note that it was shown in [46, Theorem 3.19] that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_M = 0$$\end{document}cM=0 for such a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms-module) could be the first step towards formulating a BRST quantisation procedure for the tensionless open string, or at least one of its vacua (if there exist many). It may also help formulate a generalised notion of semi-infinite cohomology for Lie algebras without integer-grading.In general, studying the restrictions and, if possible, contractions of VOA modules over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}\times {{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}$$\end{document}Vir×Vir and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}$$\end{document}Vir would provide field-theoretic insights for the tensionless open string, possibly informing us about its spectrum, quantisation, etc. Some of these modules are well-known examples of highest weight modules, so one should expect that after carefully dealing with normal-ordering ambiguities, their restriction to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathfrak {b}}$$\end{document}b^-modules does indeed give rise to the “almost free” modules discussed in Sect. 3. Overall, together with the work in Sect. 3, we expect these two strands of exploration to aid in understanding the commutative diagram given in Fig. 1 at the level of Lie algebra representations while providing new field-theoretic realisations of the BCCA, which would be very informative in deducing the possible spectra of quantised tensionless open strings.Proving Conjecture 3.17 could not only aid the search for field-theoretic realisations of the BCCA, but also result in the development of novel methods to check the (in)decomposability of Lie algebra modules and/or study representations of infinite-dimensional non-\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}Z-graded Lie algebras.A similar programme to the one in this paper can be undertaken for higher spin BCCAs [12, Equation (26)]. Following our notation in Sect. 2, the higher spin BCCAs form the one-parameter family of Lie algebras \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}\ltimes \mathcal {P}_b$$\end{document}O⋉Pb (and their central extensions), where b is usually taken to be an integer in physics, though the programme undertaken in this paper could be done for arbitrary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b \in \Bbbk $$\end{document}b∈k.One could compute the low-dimensional cohomology of the two-parameter family of higher spin BCCAs, analogous to what was done for the Lie algebras \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}(a,b) :=\mathcal {W}\ltimes I (a,b)$$\end{document}W(a,b):=W⋉I(a,b) (see Definition 2.2) in [50]. This would include a computation of the central extensions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b.One can go beyond the methods in this paper to construct modules over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathfrak {b}}$$\end{document}b^ or the centrally closed version of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b, if it is proven not to be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathfrak {b}}$$\end{document}b^. Such representations would inevitably be vital in tensionless open string theory and other fields of theoretical physics.Finally, our work can be extended to the supersymmetric versions of the BCCA. Two such algebras, dubbed the homogenous and inhomogeneous boundary superconformal Carrollian algebras (BSCCAs), were obtained in [11] via similar constructions to the ones in [12]. The study of modules over the homogenous BSCCA should serve as an invaluable stepping stone towards understanding the spectrum of the tensionless open superstring.

The BCCA can be obtained as the Lie algebra contraction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}$$\end{document}Vir (see [12, Equation (8)]). Hence, one could study \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b-modules obtained as contractions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}$$\end{document}Vir-modules. Contractions at the level of representations are essential in Carrollian physics [34], but there is still much that is unclear in this regard. For instance, the contractions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}\times {{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}$$\end{document}Vir×Vir-modules built from “mode algebras” or free fields (i.e. BC-systems, Heisenberg algebra, etc.) have been considered in the physics literature4 (see, for example, [54, Sections 5.1.2 and 5.2], [8, Section 4] and [9, Section 3.2.1]). However, there are still technical subtleties (e.g. issues with “normal-ordering”) when it comes to contracting these infinite-dimensional representations that need to be addressed. Attempting to construct sensible representations of the BCCA as contractions of representations of the Virasoro algebra may help address some of these subtleties (see, for instance, [74] for some work in this direction on VOA modules). They should also help guide us towards the quantisation of the tensionless open string since we expect such representations to describe the possible spectra of this string. It would be particularly interesting if representations constructed in this manner belong to one of the classes of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathfrak {b}}$$\end{document}b^-modules discussed in this paper.

The BCCA can be obtained as the Lie algebra contraction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}$$\end{document}Vir (see [12, Equation (8)]). Contractions at the level of representations are essential in Carrollian physics [34], but there is still much that is unclear in this regard. For instance, the contractions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}\times {{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}$$\end{document}Vir×Vir-modules built from “mode algebras” or free fields (i.e. BC-systems, Heisenberg algebra, etc.) have been considered in the physics literature4 (see, for example, [54, Sections 5.1.2 and 5.2], [8, Section 4] and [9, Section 3.2.1]). It would be particularly interesting if representations constructed in this manner belong to one of the classes of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathfrak {b}}$$\end{document}b^-modules discussed in this paper.

The above line of enquiry leads naturally to the consideration of restrictions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms-modules that are vertex operator algebra (VOA) modules [24, 41, 44] (see [84] for the first appearance of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms-modules5 in mathematics), such as the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms-module formed from two fermionic BC systems [42, 45, 48], each of conformal weight (2,-1)(2,-1). This module is also known as the semi-infinite wedge or fermionic Fock representation [42, 43], denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda ^{\boldsymbol{\cdot }}_\infty (\mathfrak {bms})$$\end{document}Λ∞·(bms). For one of the three vacua of closed tensionless string theory, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda ^{\boldsymbol{\cdot }}_\infty (\mathfrak {bms})$$\end{document}Λ∞·(bms) is the so-called ghost sector of the theory that appears during Becchi–Rouet–Stora–Tyutin (BRST) quantisation [26–28, 79]. Thus, understanding the restriction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda ^{\boldsymbol{\cdot }}_\infty (\mathfrak {bms})$$\end{document}Λ∞·(bms) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b (note that it was shown in [46, Theorem 3.19] that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_M = 0$$\end{document}cM=0 for such a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms-module) could be the first step towards formulating a BRST quantisation procedure for the tensionless open string, or at least one of its vacua (if there exist many). It may also help formulate a generalised notion of semi-infinite cohomology for Lie algebras without integer-grading.

The above line of enquiry leads naturally to the consideration of restrictions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms-modules that are vertex operator algebra (VOA) modules [24, 41, 44] (see [84] for the first appearance of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms-modules5 in mathematics), such as the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms-module formed from two fermionic BC systems [42, 45, 48], each of conformal weight (2,-1)(2,-1). This module is also known as the semi-infinite wedge or fermionic Fock representation [42, 43], denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda ^{\boldsymbol{\cdot }}_\infty (\mathfrak {bms})$$\end{document}Λ∞·(bms). For one of the three vacua of closed tensionless string theory, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda ^{\boldsymbol{\cdot }}_\infty (\mathfrak {bms})$$\end{document}Λ∞·(bms) is the so-called ghost sector of the theory that appears during Becchi–Rouet–Stora–Tyutin (BRST) quantisation [26–28, 79]. Thus, understanding the restriction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda ^{\boldsymbol{\cdot }}_\infty (\mathfrak {bms})$$\end{document}Λ∞·(bms) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b (note that it was shown in [46, Theorem 3.19] that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_M = 0$$\end{document}cM=0 for such a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms-module) could be the first step towards formulating a BRST quantisation procedure for the tensionless open string, or at least one of its vacua (if there exist many). It may also help formulate a generalised notion of semi-infinite cohomology for Lie algebras without integer-grading.

In general, studying the restrictions and, if possible, contractions of VOA modules over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {bms}$$\end{document}bms, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}\times {{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}$$\end{document}Vir×Vir and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Vir}\,}}$$\end{document}Vir would provide field-theoretic insights for the tensionless open string, possibly informing us about its spectrum, quantisation, etc. Some of these modules are well-known examples of highest weight modules, so one should expect that after carefully dealing with normal-ordering ambiguities, their restriction to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathfrak {b}}$$\end{document}b^-modules does indeed give rise to the “almost free” modules discussed in Sect. 3. Overall, together with the work in Sect. 3, we expect these two strands of exploration to aid in understanding the commutative diagram given in Fig. 1 at the level of Lie algebra representations while providing new field-theoretic realisations of the BCCA, which would be very informative in deducing the possible spectra of quantised tensionless open strings.

Overall, together with the work in Sect. 3, we expect these two strands of exploration to aid in understanding the commutative diagram given in Fig. 1 at the level of Lie algebra representations while providing new field-theoretic realisations of the BCCA, which would be very informative in deducing the possible spectra of quantised tensionless open strings.

Proving Conjecture 3.17 could not only aid the search for field-theoretic realisations of the BCCA, but also result in the development of novel methods to check the (in)decomposability of Lie algebra modules and/or study representations of infinite-dimensional non-\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}Z-graded Lie algebras.

One can go beyond the methods in this paper to construct modules over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathfrak {b}}$$\end{document}b^ or the centrally closed version of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {b}$$\end{document}b, if it is proven not to be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathfrak {b}}$$\end{document}b^. Such representations would inevitably be vital in tensionless open string theory and other fields of theoretical physics.

Such representations would inevitably be vital in tensionless open string theory and other fields of theoretical physics.

Finally, our work can be extended to the supersymmetric versions of the BCCA. Two such algebras, dubbed the homogenous and inhomogeneous boundary superconformal Carrollian algebras (BSCCAs), were obtained in [11] via similar constructions to the ones in [12]. The study of modules over the homogenous BSCCA should serve as an invaluable stepping stone towards understanding the spectrum of the tensionless open superstring.
